Original fictional case: A fictional facility review has six findings, F1-F6. Four have documented corrective-action plans and assigned owners. Of those four, F1 and F2 also have verified effectiveness evidence and are closed under the local review process. F3 and F4 remain open because their effectiveness checks are pending. F5 and F6 have neither a documented action plan nor an assigned owner. Coordinator Ruby and review lead Ian must correct a dashboard that calls all four planned findings closed. Ian will secure ownership for F5 and F6; the next status review is Thursday at 15:00, not a promise that all findings will be resolved.
Language workshop: A documented action is not automatically a closed finding
Communication goal: Report audit status using explicit evidence, ownership, and closure categories.
Language guidance: Two of six findings are closed, approximately 33.3%. Four of six have plans, approximately 66.7%. These percentages measure different states. Neither percentage alone establishes facility-wide compliance.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Four findings have plans, but only two are closed. / The review contains six findings in total. / F1 and F2 have verified effectiveness evidence. / F3 and F4 remain open pending effectiveness checks. / F5 and F6 have no assigned owner. / A documented plan is not verified closure. / The verified closure rate is two out of six. / That is approximately 33.3% of this review's findings. / Four findings remain open. / Ian will secure ownership for F5 and F6. / Thursday at 15:00 is the status review. / Link each closure to its supporting record. / An immediate correction does not automatically address recurrence. / Apply the local effectiveness-based closure process. / Do not change the closure definition to improve the dashboard. / Bring the owner updates and pending evidence to Thursday's review.
Vocabulary: compliance obligation: An applicable requirement that an organization must meet. / audit scope: The defined coverage and boundaries of an audit or review. / audit criterion: A requirement or benchmark used to evaluate evidence. / objective evidence: Verifiable information supporting a finding or conclusion. / finding: A documented result of comparing evidence with review criteria. / nonconformity: A failure to meet a specified requirement. / observation: A noted condition whose classification depends on the review process. / corrective-action plan: A documented proposal to address an identified problem and its cause. / correction: Action addressing a detected problem or instance. / corrective action: Action aimed at removing a cause to prevent recurrence. / root cause: An underlying cause relevant to preventing recurrence. / action owner: The person accountable for a specified follow-up action. / target date: A planned completion or review date. / effectiveness check: Evaluation of whether an action achieved its intended result. / closure evidence: Information supporting closure under the applicable process. / open finding: A finding not yet meeting the stated closure requirements. / overdue action: An action not completed by its applicable due date. / recurring finding: A finding involving a problem identified again. / audit trail: Records allowing actions and decisions to be traced. / evidence register: An organized record linking evidence to review items. / closure rate: The proportion of defined findings meeting the stated closure condition. / management review: A structured leadership review of relevant performance and issues. / escalation route: The defined path for raising an unresolved matter. / verification status: The recorded stage of checking a claim or action.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Sustainability and ESG ReportingA partial electricity estimate cannot support a company-wide claim+

Your goal

Define emissions-reporting boundaries and replace an unsupported headline with a precise statement.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A partial electricity estimate cannot support a company-wide claim

Sustainability analyst Ana has estimated 40 metric tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent from purchased electricity used at two offices during 2025. The calculation excludes the factory, company vehicles, purchased heat, and value-chain emissions. It has not undergone independent assurance. Communications manager Leo has drafted the headline "Our company is carbon neutral." No company-wide inventory or substantiation for that claim is available. Ana and Leo must replace the headline, name the included offices, period, and electricity source, and list the exclusions. They must not imply that omitted sources have zero emissions or that an estimate has been independently verified.

Ana: Sustainability analyst / Leo: Communications manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the 40-tonne estimate cover?

Answer and explanation

A. Purchased electricity at two offices during 2025

The calculation explicitly covers purchased electricity for two offices in the stated year.

2. Which headline is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. Our company is carbon neutral.

A partial electricity estimate does not substantiate a company-wide carbon-neutrality claim.

3. What must not be inferred from excluded sources?

Answer and explanation

C. Their emissions are zero.

Exclusion means a source was not included, not that it produced no emissions.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Scope 2 is not limited to electricity: it can include purchased or acquired steam, heat, and cooling. This case includes only electricity at two offices, not the entire company or all Scope 2 sources.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
greenhouse gas (GHG)
A gas that contributes to the atmosphere's heat-trapping effect.quantify greenhouse gas emissions
carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e)
A common unit expressing different gases using a specified warming-potential basis.report tonnes of CO2e
emissions inventory
An accounting of emissions within defined boundaries and a period.compile an emissions inventory
organizational boundary
The operations included under the chosen organizational accounting approach.define the organizational boundary
operational boundary
The categories of emissions included in an inventory.describe the operational boundary
Scope 1
Direct emissions from sources owned or controlled within the inventory boundary.report Scope 1 emissions
Scope 2
Indirect emissions from generation of purchased or acquired energy consumed by the organization.calculate Scope 2 emissions
Scope 3
Other indirect value-chain emissions within the applicable accounting framework.assess Scope 3 categories
purchased electricity
Electricity obtained from an external supplier for consumption.quantify purchased electricity use
purchased heat
Heat acquired from an external source for use.account for purchased heat
value chain
Activities upstream and downstream associated with an organization's products or operations.map the value chain
activity data
Measured or estimated activity used in calculating emissions.collect activity data
emission factor
A coefficient relating an activity to an emissions quantity.document the emission factor
global warming potential (GWP)
A measure comparing a gas's warming effect over a specified time horizon.state the GWP basis
reporting year
The period assigned to an emissions or sustainability report.specify the reporting year
base year
A reference year used for a defined comparison or target.establish a base year
location-based method
A Scope 2 method reflecting average emissions associated with the relevant grids.describe the location-based method
market-based method
A Scope 2 method using applicable contractual instruments under relevant criteria.document the market-based method
assurance
An independent evaluation of reported information under an agreed engagement.obtain independent assurance
reporting boundary
The defined extent of what a report includes.disclose the reporting boundary
carbon-neutrality claim
An assertion about balancing or neutralizing emissions under a specified substantiated basis.substantiate a carbon-neutrality claim
avoided emissions
Estimated emissions not occurring relative to a specified counterfactual.distinguish avoided emissions
carbon credit
A unit representing a specified quantified emissions reduction or removal under a program.examine carbon credit documentation
retirement
The recorded use of a credit that removes it from further use under its registry rules.verify credit retirement

CO2e aggregates gases using a specified warming-potential basis. It is not the same as electricity consumption. An inventory figure, an avoided-emissions estimate, and a carbon credit are different quantities that cannot simply replace one another.

C Notice the language

A narrower claim can be more credible

Communications teams value simple messages, while technical teams need boundaries and qualifications. Collaborate on a clear positive description of what was actually measured. Do not treat a shorter headline as permission to remove the scope that makes the statement true.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the measured activity
The estimate covers purchased electricity at two offices.
State the reporting period
The reporting year is 2025.
Give the correct unit
The estimate is 40 metric tonnes of CO2e.
Limit the boundary
This is not a company-wide emissions inventory.
Correct the headline
The evidence does not support a carbon-neutrality claim.
Preserve exclusions
The factory, vehicles, purchased heat, and value chain are excluded.
Avoid a zero assumption
Excluded does not mean zero emissions.
State assurance status
Independent assurance has not been completed.
Explain Scope 2 carefully
Purchased energy categories extend beyond electricity.
Keep methods visible
Document the calculation method and emission factors.
Separate the quantities
Electricity use and CO2e are different measures.
Avoid unsupported comparisons
We have not established a comparable base year.
Protect the claim
Keep the boundary beside the reported figure.
Avoid substitution
A credit purchase is not a complete emissions inventory.
Ask for substantiation
What evidence supports the proposed public claim?
Close accurately
Publish the limited estimate with its period, boundary, and qualifications.
6 language and precision notes
Estimated
Signals a calculated result without implying independent verification.
At two offices
Restricts the organizational coverage of this calculation.
Company-wide
A broader boundary that this evidence does not support.
Excluded
Describes omission, not the amount emitted.
Assured versus prepared
Independent evaluation is different from producing the calculation.
CO2e versus kWh
Emissions units and electricity-consumption units answer different questions.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which replacement headline is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Estimated 2025 purchased-electricity emissions for two offices: 40 tonnes CO2e.

The accurate headline preserves the activity, two-office boundary, year, estimate status, and unit.

2. Which statement about Scope 2 is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. It can include purchased heat, steam, and cooling as well as electricity.

Scope 2 covers relevant purchased or acquired energy categories, not only the electricity subset in this case.

3. What does lack of independent assurance mean here?

Answer and explanation

C. The estimate must not be described as independently assured.

No assurance engagement has established independent assurance, but its absence does not itself prove the estimate false.

4. Which question addresses the headline's main defect?

Answer and explanation

A. Does the evidence cover and substantiate the company-wide claim?

The main defect is the narrow boundary; reconciliation, unit consistency, and consultation do not extend it company-wide.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

A partial electricity estimate cannot support a company-wide claim

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ana: Sustainability analyst / Leo: Communications manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • emission factor
  • Scope 3
  • carbon credit
  • Scope 2
  • CO2e
  • Scope 1
  • reporting boundary
  • base year
  • emissions inventory
  • Assurance
  1. Leo
    The headline says our company is carbon neutral. I assumed the forty-tonne estimate covered the whole footprint.
  2. Ana
    It is a limited 1 calculation for purchased electricity at two offices in 2025. It excludes the factory, vehicles, purchased heat, and value chain. That headline is unsupported.
  3. Leo
    Understood. The calculation may be usable, but I have put a company-wide claim above a two-office number.
  4. Ana
    Exactly. Keep the 2 beside the number. We can communicate the estimate clearly without claiming that every operation or every relevant emissions source has been included.
  5. Leo
    I will name the two offices and year. The unit is tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent, not kilowatt-hours of electricity consumed.
  6. Ana
    Correct. 3 is the emissions unit. Electricity consumption is activity data used in the calculation, alongside the applicable factors and method. Keep those quantities distinct in the table and headline.
  7. Leo
    Can I say we have completed Scope 2 reporting? I thought Scope 2 was simply purchased electricity, which is what this file calculates.
  8. Ana
    4 can include purchased steam, heat, and cooling as well as electricity. This file covers only two offices and excludes purchased heat. Do not describe it as complete company-wide Scope 2 reporting.
  9. Leo
    The excluded factory and company vehicles must remain visible. Otherwise a reader could assume they were checked and contributed no emissions.
  10. Ana
    Yes. Relevant direct sources may fall within 5, depending on the organizational boundary. Excluding a source from this calculation says nothing by itself about whether that source emits zero, forty, or any other amount.
  11. Leo
    What about suppliers and customer use? Those are not included either, so our statement should not imply that we measured them.
  12. Ana
    Keep the relevant 6 boundary explicit. The applicable categories require their own assessment. A two-office electricity figure cannot stand in for upstream and downstream emissions simply because it is the number currently available.
  13. Leo
    Our team checked the arithmetic. Would verified imply independent review rather than our internal check?
  14. Ana
    No. 7 has not been completed. Say estimated and give the calculation boundary. Accurate confidence comes from describing what we have, not borrowing a status the evidence does not support.
  15. Leo
    Someone suggested purchasing forty credits and retaining the carbon-neutral headline. We have no company-wide inventory or substantiation package for that claim.
  16. Ana
    A 8 purchase would not repair the missing boundary or establish the whole claim. Its quality, use, and relevant claim requirements would need their own evidence and review.
  17. Leo
    Could we compare this estimate with last year instead? I do not have a matching calculation for the same two offices and methodology.
  18. Ana
    Then do not invent a 9 comparison. A reduction claim needs a comparable basis and supporting data. We can report the current limited estimate without claiming a trend that has not been established.
  19. Leo
    The revised headline will state estimated 2025 purchased-electricity emissions for two offices: forty metric tonnes of CO2e. The exclusions will appear immediately below.
  20. Ana
    Good. Keep the 10 and method documentation traceable in the supporting material. That gives readers a precise statement and reviewers a basis to examine, without implying company-wide neutrality or independent assurance.

Answers and explanations
  1. emissions inventory Turn 2. An emissions inventory has defined boundaries; this calculation covers only the stated electricity sources rather than the whole company.
  2. reporting boundary Turn 4. The reporting boundary states what is included, preventing a partial office estimate from becoming a company-wide assertion.
  3. CO2e Turn 6. CO2e expresses emissions on a common warming-potential basis; it is not a unit of electricity consumption.
  4. Scope 2 Turn 8. Scope 2 includes relevant purchased or acquired energy, so selected office electricity is only a subset of its potential coverage.
  5. Scope 1 Turn 10. Scope 1 concerns direct emissions from owned or controlled sources under the chosen boundary; omitted sources cannot be presumed zero.
  6. Scope 3 Turn 12. Scope 3 addresses other indirect value-chain emissions under the framework, which are outside this limited calculation.
  7. Assurance Turn 14. Assurance is an independent evaluation under an agreed engagement; internal preparation alone does not establish that status.
  8. carbon credit Turn 16. A carbon credit is a separate quantified unit under a program; purchasing it does not supply a complete inventory or substantiate every claim.
  9. base year Turn 18. A base year provides a defined comparison reference; no comparable prior-year calculation is supplied in this case.
  10. emission factor Turn 20. The emission factor links activity data to the calculated emissions and should be documented with the method and boundary.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leo: The headline says our company is carbon neutral. I assumed the forty-tonne estimate covered the whole footprint.
  2. Ana: It is a limited emissions inventory calculation for purchased electricity at two offices in 2025. It excludes the factory, vehicles, purchased heat, and value chain. That headline is unsupported.
  3. Leo: Understood. The calculation may be usable, but I have put a company-wide claim above a two-office number.
  4. Ana: Exactly. Keep the reporting boundary beside the number. We can communicate the estimate clearly without claiming that every operation or every relevant emissions source has been included.
  5. Leo: I will name the two offices and year. The unit is tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent, not kilowatt-hours of electricity consumed.
  6. Ana: Correct. CO2e is the emissions unit. Electricity consumption is activity data used in the calculation, alongside the applicable factors and method. Keep those quantities distinct in the table and headline.
  7. Leo: Can I say we have completed Scope 2 reporting? I thought Scope 2 was simply purchased electricity, which is what this file calculates.
  8. Ana: Scope 2 can include purchased steam, heat, and cooling as well as electricity. This file covers only two offices and excludes purchased heat. Do not describe it as complete company-wide Scope 2 reporting.
  9. Leo: The excluded factory and company vehicles must remain visible. Otherwise a reader could assume they were checked and contributed no emissions.
  10. Ana: Yes. Relevant direct sources may fall within Scope 1, depending on the organizational boundary. Excluding a source from this calculation says nothing by itself about whether that source emits zero, forty, or any other amount.
  11. Leo: What about suppliers and customer use? Those are not included either, so our statement should not imply that we measured them.
  12. Ana: Keep the relevant Scope 3 boundary explicit. The applicable categories require their own assessment. A two-office electricity figure cannot stand in for upstream and downstream emissions simply because it is the number currently available.
  13. Leo: Our team checked the arithmetic. Would verified imply independent review rather than our internal check?
  14. Ana: No. Assurance has not been completed. Say estimated and give the calculation boundary. Accurate confidence comes from describing what we have, not borrowing a status the evidence does not support.
  15. Leo: Someone suggested purchasing forty credits and retaining the carbon-neutral headline. We have no company-wide inventory or substantiation package for that claim.
  16. Ana: A carbon credit purchase would not repair the missing boundary or establish the whole claim. Its quality, use, and relevant claim requirements would need their own evidence and review.
  17. Leo: Could we compare this estimate with last year instead? I do not have a matching calculation for the same two offices and methodology.
  18. Ana: Then do not invent a base year comparison. A reduction claim needs a comparable basis and supporting data. We can report the current limited estimate without claiming a trend that has not been established.
  19. Leo: The revised headline will state estimated 2025 purchased-electricity emissions for two offices: forty metric tonnes of CO2e. The exclusions will appear immediately below.
  20. Ana: Good. Keep the emission factor and method documentation traceable in the supporting material. That gives readers a precise statement and reviewers a basis to examine, without implying company-wide neutrality or independent assurance.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Selected operations do not support a company-wide claim

Fictional sustainability review: a carbon-neutral claim is drafted, but the supporting calculation covers selected operations only; no certification or claim validity is established.

  1. Sustainability communications manager: The draft calls the company carbon neutral, but the calculation covers only selected operations.
  2. ESG analyst: Then the claim exceeds the evidence boundary. We need to identify exactly what was included.
  3. Sustainability communications manager: Could we keep the headline and put the limited scope in a small note below?
  4. ESG analyst: A broad headline would still mislead. The main wording should reflect the actual assessed boundary.
  5. Sustainability communications manager: The calculation mentions Scope 1 and Scope 2. Does that establish all Scope 3 emissions too?
  6. ESG analyst: No. Those categories aren't interchangeable, and we must not infer coverage absent from the calculation.
  7. Sustainability communications manager: I'll remove the company-wide conclusion and request the qualified review of any narrower proposed claim.
  8. ESG analyst: Good. A limited calculation alone doesn't establish that even the narrower carbon-neutral wording is justified.
  9. Sustainability communications manager: We'll report what was calculated and identify unassessed operations rather than announce a verified status.
  10. ESG analyst: That keeps the ESG communication tied to evidence instead of using an unsupported environmental label.
A smaller footprint may reflect a smaller boundary

Separate fictional emissions comparison: this year's inventory excludes a sold facility that was included last year; a like-for-like comparison has not been prepared.

  1. Sustainability director: Our reported emissions fell. Can we say every operation became more efficient this year?
  2. Inventory analyst: The reporting boundary changed: this year excludes a sold facility that last year's inventory included.
  3. Sustainability director: So the lower total may partly reflect fewer operations rather than improved performance at each site.
  4. Inventory analyst: Exactly. We need a consistent comparison before attributing the change to efficiency measures.
  5. Sustainability director: Would combining Scope 1 and Scope 2 totals remove the effect of that boundary change?
  6. Inventory analyst: No. Adding categories doesn't restore the missing facility to the comparison or establish like-for-like performance.
  7. Sustainability director: I'll label the boundary change and ask for an analysis using comparable operations where appropriate.
  8. Inventory analyst: Good. Keep any Scope 3 questions separate and don't imply those emissions were assessed here.
  9. Sustainability director: The ESG update will report the observed total and its changed coverage, not universal operational improvement.
  10. Inventory analyst: That allows readers to understand the reduction without drawing a stronger conclusion than the inventory supports.
A supplier survey is not verified emissions data

Separate fictional ESG case: thirty suppliers answered a questionnaire, but their emissions figures have not been checked and the remaining suppliers have not responded.

  1. Procurement sustainability lead: Thirty suppliers responded. Can we call our Scope 3 data collection complete and verified?
  2. ESG data reviewer: No. Other suppliers haven't responded, and the submitted figures haven't been checked yet.
  3. Procurement sustainability lead: Then the response count shows participation, not complete coverage or reliable totals for the inventory.
  4. ESG data reviewer: Correct. We should report the responses received, missing coverage, and verification status separately.
  5. Procurement sustainability lead: Could unanswered questionnaires be entered as zero emissions so the reporting table can be finished?
  6. ESG data reviewer: No. Missing data aren't evidence of zero emissions; the appropriate methodology must address gaps transparently.
  7. Procurement sustainability lead: I'll ask the reporting specialist how to handle missing information rather than invent numerical values.
  8. ESG data reviewer: Good. Also preserve the suppliers' stated boundaries and assumptions when reviewing their submissions.
  9. Procurement sustainability lead: I'll revise the update to thirty responses received, with coverage and data-quality review still open.
  10. ESG data reviewer: That is a defensible collection status, not an unsupported claim of a verified Scope 3 inventory.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A partial electricity estimate cannot support a company-wide claim
  1. Read turns 1-10, keeping the two-office boundary attached to forty tonnes CO2e.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20, distinguishing internal calculation from independent assurance.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Narrow another emissions headline

A calculation estimates 12 tonnes CO2e from purchased electricity at one warehouse in 2024. Fuel use and transport are excluded. Independent assurance has not been completed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The estimated emissions are ___ tonnes CO2e."

Answer and explanation

D. 12

Twelve is the emissions quantity supplied for this separate limited calculation.

2. Editor: "The included location is one ___."

Answer and explanation

B. warehouse

The calculation covers one warehouse, not every company operation.

3. Analyst: "The reporting year is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 2024

The supplied reporting period is 2024 and must remain attached to the figure.

4. Editor: "Independent assurance has ___ been completed."

Answer and explanation

B. not

The brief explicitly states that independent assurance has not been completed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Sustainability and ESG Reporting
Original fictional case: Sustainability analyst Ana has estimated 40 metric tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent from purchased electricity used at two offices during 2025. The calculation excludes the factory, company vehicles, purchased heat, and value-chain emissions. It has not undergone independent assurance. Communications manager Leo has drafted the headline "Our company is carbon neutral." No company-wide inventory or substantiation for that claim is available. Ana and Leo must replace the headline, name the included offices, period, and electricity source, and list the exclusions. They must not imply that omitted sources have zero emissions or that an estimate has been independently verified.
Language workshop: A partial electricity estimate cannot support a company-wide claim
Communication goal: Define emissions-reporting boundaries and replace an unsupported headline with a precise statement.
Language guidance: Scope 2 is not limited to electricity: it can include purchased or acquired steam, heat, and cooling. This case includes only electricity at two offices, not the entire company or all Scope 2 sources.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The estimate covers purchased electricity at two offices. / The reporting year is 2025. / The estimate is 40 metric tonnes of CO2e. / This is not a company-wide emissions inventory. / The evidence does not support a carbon-neutrality claim. / The factory, vehicles, purchased heat, and value chain are excluded. / Excluded does not mean zero emissions. / Independent assurance has not been completed. / Purchased energy categories extend beyond electricity. / Document the calculation method and emission factors. / Electricity use and CO2e are different measures. / We have not established a comparable base year. / Keep the boundary beside the reported figure. / A credit purchase is not a complete emissions inventory. / What evidence supports the proposed public claim? / Publish the limited estimate with its period, boundary, and qualifications.
Vocabulary: greenhouse gas (GHG): A gas that contributes to the atmosphere's heat-trapping effect. / carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e): A common unit expressing different gases using a specified warming-potential basis. / emissions inventory: An accounting of emissions within defined boundaries and a period. / organizational boundary: The operations included under the chosen organizational accounting approach. / operational boundary: The categories of emissions included in an inventory. / Scope 1: Direct emissions from sources owned or controlled within the inventory boundary. / Scope 2: Indirect emissions from generation of purchased or acquired energy consumed by the organization. / Scope 3: Other indirect value-chain emissions within the applicable accounting framework. / purchased electricity: Electricity obtained from an external supplier for consumption. / purchased heat: Heat acquired from an external source for use. / value chain: Activities upstream and downstream associated with an organization's products or operations. / activity data: Measured or estimated activity used in calculating emissions. / emission factor: A coefficient relating an activity to an emissions quantity. / global warming potential (GWP): A measure comparing a gas's warming effect over a specified time horizon. / reporting year: The period assigned to an emissions or sustainability report. / base year: A reference year used for a defined comparison or target. / location-based method: A Scope 2 method reflecting average emissions associated with the relevant grids. / market-based method: A Scope 2 method using applicable contractual instruments under relevant criteria. / assurance: An independent evaluation of reported information under an agreed engagement. / reporting boundary: The defined extent of what a report includes. / carbon-neutrality claim: An assertion about balancing or neutralizing emissions under a specified substantiated basis. / avoided emissions: Estimated emissions not occurring relative to a specified counterfactual. / carbon credit: A unit representing a specified quantified emissions reduction or removal under a program. / retirement: The recorded use of a credit that removes it from further use under its registry rules.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, keeping the two-office boundary attached to forty tonnes CO2e. Swap roles for turns 11-20, distinguishing internal calculation from independent assurance. Complete the warehouse transfer and check location, year, quantity, and assurance status.
Partner: Ana: Sustainability analyst / Leo: Communications manager

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Community and Stakeholder MeetingsAnswer a safety question without extending the evidence+

Your goal

Acknowledge residents and explain preliminary results in plain language with precise boundaries.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Answer a safety question without extending the evidence

Consultant Suki and community representative Daniel are preparing a public meeting about a fictional environmental study. Preliminary laboratory results cover water samples from locations S1, S2, and S3 collected on one June morning and tested for the listed analytes. For analyte X, each result is below the laboratory reporting limit. Other locations, other dates, and unlisted substances are outside these results. The study has not established area-wide safety or a drinking-water determination. Suki will explain these limits, record questions, and direct requests for current health or water-use advice to the responsible public authority without inventing advice.

Daniel: Community representative / Suki: Environmental consultant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the sampling cover?

Answer and explanation

C. Three locations on one June morning

The brief limits the results to three locations, one collection period, and the listed analytes.

2. What does below the reporting limit establish?

Answer and explanation

C. The laboratory did not report a quantitative value at or above that limit for X.

A below-limit result is tied to the laboratory reporting basis and does not prove universal absence or safety.

3. Where should requests for current health or water-use advice go?

Answer and explanation

A. The responsible public authority

The responsible authority provides current advice; study summaries, analytical reporting limits, and meeting consensus do not replace it.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Below the reporting limit does not mean zero. It also does not describe untested substances, places, or dates. Distinguish the laboratory statement from a conclusion about health or allowable water use.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
stakeholder
A person or group affected by or interested in a project.engage a stakeholder
community liaison
A contact connecting a project team with affected community members.appoint a community liaison
public meeting
A gathering for sharing information and receiving public questions.prepare a public meeting
sampling location
The place where a sample was collected.identify the sampling location
sampling event
A defined occasion or period of sample collection.describe the sampling event
analyte
A substance or property targeted by an analysis.list the target analytes
preliminary result
A result not yet at the final review or reporting stage.qualify a preliminary result
non-detect
A result indicating an analyte was not detected at the stated analytical threshold.explain a non-detect
quantitation
Determination of an amount or concentration under the method's conditions.describe the quantitation limit
screening level
A comparison value used to identify matters needing further evaluation.interpret a screening level
cleanup level
An applicable concentration or criterion used in a defined cleanup context.identify the applicable cleanup level
exposure assessment
Evaluation of how, how much, and under what conditions exposure may occur.conduct an exposure assessment
spatial coverage
The geographic extent represented by the data.explain spatial coverage
temporal coverage
The period or times represented by the data.explain temporal coverage
representativeness
The extent to which data reflect the conditions relevant to a question.evaluate representativeness
uncertainty
A limitation in knowledge or confidence about a result or inference.communicate uncertainty
risk communication
Exchange of information about risks, evidence, and relevant uncertainties.improve risk communication
plain-language summary
An account using accessible words while preserving the meaning.prepare a plain-language summary
question log
A record of questions and their follow-up status.maintain a question log
responsible authority
The body with the relevant responsibility for the matter.contact the responsible authority
public advisory
An official notice giving relevant information or guidance to the public.check the current public advisory
meeting record
Documentation of discussion, questions, and commitments.circulate the meeting record
follow-up contact
A named person or channel for subsequent communication.provide a follow-up contact
area-wide claim
A statement applied to an entire geographic area.qualify an area-wide claim

A screening level identifies a need for further evaluation in context; it is not automatically a cleanup standard or a universal safe/unsafe boundary. This fictional case supplies no threshold for a health determination.

C Notice the language

Answer the human question as well as the technical one

Residents are asking about daily decisions, not merely laboratory vocabulary. Acknowledge that practical concern before explaining the evidence. Avoid using uncertainty as a reason to withdraw from the conversation or using reassurance as a substitute for a qualified answer.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
I understand that you need information for everyday decisions.
State the sampling coverage
These results cover S1, S2, and S3 on one June morning.
Name the tested substance
This statement concerns analyte X.
Explain the result
X was below the laboratory reporting limit in these samples.
Avoid an absence claim
Below the limit does not mean proven zero.
Preserve the stage
These results are preliminary.
Limit the geography
We cannot extend them to every location in the area.
Limit the time
One sampling event does not represent every season.
Keep untested substances separate
The report does not characterize substances that were not analyzed.
Avoid a health guarantee
This study has not established an area-wide safety determination.
Answer respectfully
That is a reasonable question, but these results do not answer it fully.
Refer the advice question
Please consult the responsible public authority for current water-use advice.
Avoid inventing guidance
I will not create a drinking-water instruction from this chart.
Record the question
We will include that request in the question log.
Provide continuity
The meeting record will identify the follow-up contact.
Close with the evidence
We will distinguish the measured findings from the questions still open.
6 language and precision notes
These samples
Keeps a statement attached to its actual evidence.
Below the limit
Does not mean absent, harmless, or unmeasurable by every method.
Preliminary
Names the reporting stage without declaring the result false.
Everywhere and always
Expand spatial and temporal coverage beyond this study.
Current advice
Requires the responsible authority's actual guidance, not a guessed instruction.
Does not answer fully
Acknowledges a relevant question without manufacturing certainty.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which public statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. X was below the reporting limit in these three preliminary samples.

The supported statement preserves the analyte, sample count, analytical limit, and preliminary status.

2. Which phrase improperly expands time coverage?

Answer and explanation

B. The area is safe in every season.

Every season claims evidence beyond the single collection period supplied in the case.

3. How should Suki respond to a drinking-water question?

Answer and explanation

A. Acknowledge the concern and refer current advice to the responsible authority.

The study does not establish drinking-water advice, so Suki should acknowledge the question and use the appropriate referral.

4. Which comparison is correct?

Answer and explanation

B. Screening levels and cleanup levels serve different purposes.

Screening values support further evaluation and are not automatically the applicable cleanup criteria or a health determination.

E Conversations

Answer a safety question without extending the evidence

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Daniel: Community representative / Suki: Environmental consultant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • question log
  • temporal coverage
  • follow-up contact
  • screening level
  • plain-language summary
  • analyte
  • preliminary result
  • spatial coverage
  • risk communication
  • responsible authority
  1. Daniel
    Residents will ask whether the results prove the whole area is safe. They need to know what this means for their families.
  2. Suki
    I understand that concern. Start with the actual 1: locations S1, S2, and S3. We should not imply that these three points represent every location or every possible exposure in the area.
  3. Daniel
    The chart shows X below the reporting limit at all three locations. Some readers will interpret that as no X anywhere.
  4. Suki
    Explain the 2 and the limit together. The laboratory did not report a quantitative value at or above its reporting limit for X in these samples. That is not proof of zero.
  5. Daniel
    Those limits may sound evasive. Can you explain the result briefly without calling the whole area clean?
  6. Suki
    Use a 3, but avoid clean as a blanket label. Say what was tested, where, and when. Then explain that the result does not establish conditions at untested locations or for unlisted substances.
  7. Daniel
    We should name the June collection date. Otherwise the chart could appear to describe the whole year.
  8. Suki
    Exactly. The 4 is limited. One sampling event does not show seasonal variation or every later condition. A clear date helps readers understand what the study actually observed.
  9. Daniel
    If someone asks whether they can drink water from a particular source, can we answer from these results alone?
  10. Suki
    No. We need to identify the 5 for current health or water-use advice. These preliminary study results have not established a drinking-water determination. We must not improvise one during the meeting.
  11. Daniel
    Please give us a named contact, not just 'ask the authorities.' Families will want to know where their unanswered question is going.
  12. Suki
    Provide the appropriate 6 and record the question. Explain why the study does not answer it, and distinguish that limitation from unwillingness to listen or help them locate the relevant guidance.
  13. Daniel
    Someone may bring a screening-level table from another report. Should we tell them any number below that table is automatically safe?
  14. Suki
    No. A 7 has a defined purpose and assumptions. It is not automatically a cleanup standard or a universal dividing line between safe and unsafe for every place, substance, and exposure.
  15. Daniel
    The meeting materials should also make clear that the results are preliminary. Does that mean we should avoid discussing them at all?
  16. Suki
    We can discuss each 8 accurately. State the stage and limitations, and avoid final claims it cannot support. Withholding every explanation would leave readers to supply their own, possibly broader interpretation.
  17. Daniel
    I will organize the questions by topic and identify which ones the study can answer and which need a separate response.
  18. Suki
    Maintain a 9 with the follow-up status. Do not mark a question answered merely because someone spoke about a related issue. The record should show the actual response or remaining need.
  19. Daniel
    Our closing message will give the three locations, one June morning, listed analytes, below-limit result for X, and the limits on wider conclusions.
  20. Suki
    Good. Keep 10 both precise and respectful. We can acknowledge the practical concern, explain the evidence, and provide a route for further advice without claiming that this limited dataset establishes area-wide safety.

Answers and explanations
  1. spatial coverage Turn 2. Spatial coverage identifies represented places; three sampling locations do not characterize the whole area.
  2. analyte Turn 4. Analyte names the tested substance; the result concerns X rather than every possible substance.
  3. plain-language summary Turn 6. A plain-language summary simplifies wording without replacing the result's limits with a misleading label.
  4. temporal coverage Turn 8. Temporal coverage identifies represented times; one morning does not establish conditions throughout the year.
  5. responsible authority Turn 10. The responsible authority provides current advice; the consultant must not invent guidance from these limited results.
  6. follow-up contact Turn 12. A follow-up contact offers further communication without implying that the study answers the water-use question.
  7. screening level Turn 14. A screening level supports further evaluation, not an automatic cleanup criterion or universal safety determination.
  8. preliminary result Turn 16. A preliminary result can be explained with its stage and limits; preliminary does not mean false.
  9. question log Turn 18. The question log keeps community concerns and their follow-up status visible, including unresolved questions.
  10. risk communication Turn 20. Risk communication combines accurate evidence and uncertainty with respectful attention to affected people's practical concerns.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Daniel: Residents will ask whether the results prove the whole area is safe. They need to know what this means for their families.
  2. Suki: I understand that concern. Start with the actual spatial coverage: locations S1, S2, and S3. We should not imply that these three points represent every location or every possible exposure in the area.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A sampled location is not the whole area

Fictional community-meeting preparation: preliminary sampling covers specified locations and conditions; no area-wide safety conclusion is supplied.

  1. Community liaison: Residents ask whether the preliminary results prove the entire area is safe for everyone.
  2. Environmental project lead: The results concern the sampled locations and conditions, not every place or possible exposure.
  3. Community liaison: How can I explain that without making the information sound useless to the stakeholder group?
  4. Environmental project lead: Say what was sampled and what the results describe, then explain what lies outside that scope.
  5. Community liaison: Could I compare it to checking selected points rather than inspecting every part of the area?
  6. Environmental project lead: Yes, provided the comparison doesn't imply a technical conclusion the qualified assessment hasn't established.
  7. Community liaison: Should we call any untested location dangerous because we cannot provide an area-wide reassurance?
  8. Environmental project lead: No. Untested means information is missing, not that either safety or danger has been established.
  9. Community liaison: I'll acknowledge the concern and direct location-specific exposure questions to the qualified review team.
  10. Environmental project lead: Good risk communication explains useful evidence and its limits without replacing uncertainty with reassurance or alarm.
A plume map is not a personal exposure record

Separate fictional stakeholder briefing: a preliminary map shows a modeled plume extent, but individual exposure information is not available.

  1. Stakeholder meeting facilitator: People may think the plume map shows exactly what each resident has been exposed to.
  2. Environmental scientist: It doesn't. This preliminary map represents a modeled distribution, not an individual's measured exposure history.
  3. Stakeholder meeting facilitator: Could you explain the distinction in words that residents can repeat without technical training?
  4. Environmental scientist: A map can indicate where a substance may be distributed; personal exposure depends on additional information.
  5. Stakeholder meeting facilitator: Then living near a shaded area doesn't itself establish a particular health outcome for someone.
  6. Environmental scientist: Correct. We must avoid making personal health conclusions from this preliminary environmental map.
  7. Stakeholder meeting facilitator: Should I ask the team to explain the map boundary, assumptions, and remaining uncertainty before questions?
  8. Environmental scientist: Yes. Those details help stakeholders understand what the figure does and doesn't show.
  9. Stakeholder meeting facilitator: I'll invite specific concerns and route personal exposure questions to the appropriate qualified professionals.
  10. Environmental scientist: Good. The meeting should clarify the evidence, not use a diagram as a substitute for individual assessment.
A screening value is not a complete risk conclusion

Separate fictional risk-communication rehearsal: a table compares preliminary results with a screening value; the qualified risk assessment remains open.

  1. Client communications officer: The table includes a screening value. Can I describe that number as the boundary between safe and unsafe?
  2. Risk communication specialist: That would oversimplify its role. We need the qualified reviewer to explain its purpose and applicability.
  3. Client communications officer: How can I introduce the table without giving the stakeholder audience a misleading shortcut?
  4. Risk communication specialist: Describe it as part of the review, not a complete determination of every exposure or risk.
  5. Client communications officer: Then the comparison supports questions for assessment rather than a universal conclusion about the entire site.
  6. Risk communication specialist: Exactly. Sampling scope, conditions, and the relevant interpretation all matter to the actual conclusion.
  7. Client communications officer: I'll avoid saying a single number proves no concern or confirms harm for every resident.
  8. Risk communication specialist: Good. Ask the reviewer to explain the applicable meaning in plain language before the public meeting.
  9. Client communications officer: I'll also state that the risk assessment is ongoing and collect questions about the reviewed locations.
  10. Risk communication specialist: That keeps the discussion useful while leaving technical and health conclusions with the appropriate qualified review.

Two scenarios to practice

Answer a safety question without extending the evidence
  1. Read turns 1-10, acknowledging the concern before stating the sampling limits.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish a screening value from a health determination.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Limit another public summary

A preliminary soil report covers locations P1 and P2 sampled on 8 May. It reports analyte Y only. The report does not characterize other locations, dates, or substances.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Consultant: "The report covers ___ locations."

Answer and explanation

C. two

P1 and P2 are the two locations explicitly included in the report.

2. Resident: "The collection date was ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 8 May

Eight May is the supplied sampling date, not a claim about other periods.

3. Consultant: "The reported analyte is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Y

Y is the only analyte included in this separate report.

4. Resident: "The results are still ___."

Answer and explanation

D. preliminary

The supplied report is preliminary and must not be presented as a final determination.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 07: Community and Stakeholder Meetings
Original fictional case: Consultant Suki and community representative Daniel are preparing a public meeting about a fictional environmental study. Preliminary laboratory results cover water samples from locations S1, S2, and S3 collected on one June morning and tested for the listed analytes. For analyte X, each result is below the laboratory reporting limit. Other locations, other dates, and unlisted substances are outside these results. The study has not established area-wide safety or a drinking-water determination. Suki will explain these limits, record questions, and direct requests for current health or water-use advice to the responsible public authority without inventing advice.
Language workshop: Answer a safety question without extending the evidence
Communication goal: Acknowledge residents and explain preliminary results in plain language with precise boundaries.
Language guidance: Below the reporting limit does not mean zero. It also does not describe untested substances, places, or dates. Distinguish the laboratory statement from a conclusion about health or allowable water use.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that you need information for everyday decisions. / These results cover S1, S2, and S3 on one June morning. / This statement concerns analyte X. / X was below the laboratory reporting limit in these samples. / Below the limit does not mean proven zero. / These results are preliminary. / We cannot extend them to every location in the area. / One sampling event does not represent every season. / The report does not characterize substances that were not analyzed. / This study has not established an area-wide safety determination. / That is a reasonable question, but these results do not answer it fully. / Please consult the responsible public authority for current water-use advice. / I will not create a drinking-water instruction from this chart. / We will include that request in the question log. / The meeting record will identify the follow-up contact. / We will distinguish the measured findings from the questions still open.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Suki: Maintain a question log with the follow-up status. Do not mark a question answered merely because someone spoke about a related issue. The record should show the actual response or remaining need.
Daniel: Our closing message will give the three locations, one June morning, listed analytes, below-limit result for X, and the limits on wider conclusions.
Suki: Good. Keep risk communication both precise and respectful. We can acknowledge the practical concern, explain the evidence, and provide a route for further advice without claiming that this limited dataset establishes area-wide safety.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Community and Stakeholder Meetings
Original fictional case: Consultant Suki and community representative Daniel are preparing a public meeting about a fictional environmental study. Preliminary laboratory results cover water samples from locations S1, S2, and S3 collected on one June morning and tested for the listed analytes. For analyte X, each result is below the laboratory reporting limit. Other locations, other dates, and unlisted substances are outside these results. The study has not established area-wide safety or a drinking-water determination. Suki will explain these limits, record questions, and direct requests for current health or water-use advice to the responsible public authority without inventing advice.
Language workshop: Answer a safety question without extending the evidence
Communication goal: Acknowledge residents and explain preliminary results in plain language with precise boundaries.
Language guidance: Below the reporting limit does not mean zero. It also does not describe untested substances, places, or dates. Distinguish the laboratory statement from a conclusion about health or allowable water use.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that you need information for everyday decisions. / These results cover S1, S2, and S3 on one June morning. / This statement concerns analyte X. / X was below the laboratory reporting limit in these samples. / Below the limit does not mean proven zero. / These results are preliminary. / We cannot extend them to every location in the area. / One sampling event does not represent every season. / The report does not characterize substances that were not analyzed. / This study has not established an area-wide safety determination. / That is a reasonable question, but these results do not answer it fully. / Please consult the responsible public authority for current water-use advice. / I will not create a drinking-water instruction from this chart. / We will include that request in the question log. / The meeting record will identify the follow-up contact. / We will distinguish the measured findings from the questions still open.
Vocabulary: stakeholder: A person or group affected by or interested in a project. / community liaison: A contact connecting a project team with affected community members. / public meeting: A gathering for sharing information and receiving public questions. / sampling location: The place where a sample was collected. / sampling event: A defined occasion or period of sample collection. / analyte: A substance or property targeted by an analysis. / preliminary result: A result not yet at the final review or reporting stage. / non-detect: A result indicating an analyte was not detected at the stated analytical threshold. / quantitation: Determination of an amount or concentration under the method's conditions. / screening level: A comparison value used to identify matters needing further evaluation. / cleanup level: An applicable concentration or criterion used in a defined cleanup context. / exposure assessment: Evaluation of how, how much, and under what conditions exposure may occur. / spatial coverage: The geographic extent represented by the data. / temporal coverage: The period or times represented by the data. / representativeness: The extent to which data reflect the conditions relevant to a question. / uncertainty: A limitation in knowledge or confidence about a result or inference. / risk communication: Exchange of information about risks, evidence, and relevant uncertainties. / plain-language summary: An account using accessible words while preserving the meaning. / question log: A record of questions and their follow-up status. / responsible authority: The body with the relevant responsibility for the matter. / public advisory: An official notice giving relevant information or guidance to the public. / meeting record: Documentation of discussion, questions, and commitments. / follow-up contact: A named person or channel for subsequent communication. / area-wide claim: A statement applied to an entire geographic area.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, acknowledging the concern before stating the sampling limits. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish a screening value from a health determination. Complete the soil-report transfer and check the locations, date, analyte, and stage.
Partner: Daniel: Community representative / Suki: Environmental consultant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A preliminary soil report covers locations P1 and P2 sampled on 8 May. It reports analyte Y only. The report does not characterize other locations, dates, or substances.

Daniel: Our closing message will give the three locations, one June morning, listed analytes, below-limit result for X, and the limits on wider conclusions.
Suki: Good. Keep risk communication both precise and respectful. We can acknowledge the practical concern, explain the evidence, and provide a route for further advice without claiming that this limited dataset establishes area-wide safety.
